<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a clear picture:

1. **scale_69b_a100_s43_fp32** (GPU 3): Just loaded model weights, starting Generation 0 baseline. It's in early stage.

2. **interv_react_s43** (GPU 0): The log shows it's at "Generation 2/10, Generating synthetic data..." — **this is a RESTART**. The old gen directories (gen_0 through gen_10) are from a previous killed run. The current restarted process is now at gen 2 (or about to start gen 2 training), but it's re-computing MAUVE metrics first (gen_1 ppl=55.59, entropy=1.7930 — which is very different from the gen_10 metrics in the old run's gen_10/metrics.json: token_entropy=3.088, distinct_1=0.083, ece=0.015).
   
So the directory actually contains mixed data from both runs — the new process has overwritten gen_0 and gen_1, while gen_2 through gen_10 still hold the old run's results. As the restarted process continues, it'll progressively overwrite each generation directory as it reaches it.

Looking at the interv_react logs more closely, gen_1 shows extremely low entropy at 1.793 compared to the baseline of 3.234, and the process kicked off about 20 minutes ago. The intervention mode is reactive starting at α=1.0, triggering at gen_2 where it drops to α=0.0 with 100% synthetic data — the model's already in severe collapse by that point.

I need to clarify something crucial: the gen_10/metrics.json I examined earlier is from the old killed run, not the current one. The current process is only at gen_2, so those token_entropy readings at gen_10 showing recovery are stale data from the previous attempt. The new run should follow a similar trajectory if it completes, but I'm looking at outdated information. The logs show the training is mid-generation with about 4300/5000 steps completed. Looking at the metrics output, I can see gen 1 wrapped up with those final scores, and now gen 2 is starting to generate synthetic data. For the other experiments, t20_a025_s42_fp32 just finished gen_0 metrics and is beginning gen 1 out of 20 total generations, while a065_s42_fp32 appears to be at a similar early stage.

Now I'm identifying some critical issues: the registry shows a data integrity problem where experiments that were killed and restarted still have "conclusion=killed" even though their status is back to RUNNING — this needs to be cleared. More concerning is that interv_react_s43 has stale directories from gen_2 through gen_10 left over from the previous killed run, which could cause conflicts if the current run tries to overwrite them.

For disk space, I'm checking whether we have enough capacity: the Pythia-6.9B experiment alone needs about 26GB, and the other three concurrent experiments need roughly 13GB combined, totaling around 39GB of new data. With 54GB free on westd-17855, that leaves about 15GB margin, which is tight but workable. The scale_69b experiment on C007 is the tightest constraint at 52GB (double the estimate), leaving almost no buffer if we run everything simultaneously.

Meanwhile, westd-19828 has two idle GPUs with 96GB each while westd-17855 is straining under four experiments. Some of the smaller Pythia-410m runs could've been distributed to 19828 to ease the disk pressure, though that depends on its current usage against its 250GB total capacity.

Before I draft a report for the Director, I need to verify that the experiments being launched actually match the approved action matrix and experiment plan. Looking at the first one, `t20_a025_s42_fp32` corresponds to the T=20, α=0.25 configuration that was in the pending list.

Checking the others: `alpha_sweep_a065_s42_fp32` fills the α=0.65 gap between the sync dip point and α=0.75, `scale_69b_a100_s43_fp32` is the Pythia 6.9B scale generalization check with seed 43, and `interv_react_s43` appears to be the reactive intervention experiment for the mitigation story. All of these align with the action matrix priorities.

Now I'm drafting the report with the key issues: there's a registry inconsistency where two experiments show conclusion="killed" but still have active processes running, suggesting they were restarted without clearing the old state, and `interv_react_s43` has stale generation directories from a previous killed run coexisting with the new ones.

Beyond those, disk space on westd-17855 is tight at 54GB free against ~39GB of concurrent new experiments, which barely meets the C007 threshold individually but the aggregate demand is concerning. Meanwhile westd-19828 has two idle GPUs that could absorb some Pythia-410m experiments to relieve pressure on 17855. The four actively training experiments are all properly aligned with priorities.

Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). {
  "raw": "结果已注册。总结：\n\n**数据验证**：T=10 和 T=20 的 s42 数据与任务表完全一致。多 seed 有偏差（α=0.10 s43=3/8; α=0.75 s44=0/8 可能是 19828 未 re-analyze）。\n\n**拟合结果（Binomial MLE）**：\n\n| 参数 | Sigmoid T=10 | Sigmoid Combined | 变化 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| **k** | 478.0 ± 474.5 | 279.3 ± 527.6 | **-41.6%** |\n| **α_c** | 0.4826 | 0.4512 | **-0.031 左移** |\n| **y_max** | 0.750 | 0.750 | 不变 |\n| **90% 过渡宽度** | 0.009 | 0.016 | **+72%** |\n\n**模型比较**：\n- Sigmoid ≈ Probit（AIC 完全一致：T=10 = 32.99，combined = 79.86），三个模型的 NLL 相同（13.496 / 36.929）\n- Gen logistic 多 1 参数无改善，AIC 被惩罚 +2\n- **所有模型不可区分**——数据本质上是阶跃函数，k 的 SE ≈ k 本身\n\n**关键解读**：\n- T=10 数据是 α≈0.48 处的近完美阶跃函数\n- T=20 α=0.45=5/8 是唯一的亚平台非零点，迫使过渡变缓、α_c 左移\n- 但即使加入 T=20，过渡仍然很陡（宽度仅 0.016），需要更多中间 α 数据点才能约束过渡形状"
}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me review this sigmoid steepness analysis:

**Key findings:**

1. **Data used**: 
   - T=10: 11 α points (s42 only, excluding α=0.60 sync dip)
   - T=20: 4 α points (excl α=0.60)
   - Combined: 15 data points

2. **Sigmoid fit results**:
   - T=10 only: k=478 ± 475, α_c=0.483, y_max=0.750
   - Combined: k=279 ± 528, α_c=0.451, y_max=0.750
   - Adding T=20 data flattens the transition significantly, with the critical point shifting left by about 0.03

3. **Model comparison**:
   - Sigmoid and probit models are essentially equivalent
   - Generalized logistic adds complexity without improving fit
   - The data itself behaves like a sharp step function, so all models converge to the same solution

4. **Critical issue**: The steepness parameter has enormous uncertainty—the standard error is nearly as large as the estimate itself, making the k value highly unreliable

. This reveals the fundamental problem: the data lacks sufficient resolution around the transition zone to pin down how gradual or sharp the sigmoid actually is. The 90% transition spans only 0.009 to 0.016 units, which is too narrow to constrain the slope meaningfully.

5. **Data quality concerns**: Some workers show inconsistent results across seeds, and at least one data point may not have been properly reanalyzed, which could be introducing noise into the estimates.

The core takeaway is that while the statistical analysis is sound, the underlying data simply doesn't contain enough information to reliably estimate steepness—the parameter is essentially floating free.

2. **The α_c shift is driven by a single outlier**: The leftward shift from 0.483 to 0.451 hinges almost entirely on one T=20 point at α=0.45 showing 5/8 success versus 0/8 at T=10. That single observation in the transition zone is so constraining that it produces an implausibly small standard error of 0.0005, which reflects overfitting to that one data point rather than genuine precision. The conclusion about the critical point is fragile and shouldn't be presented as robust.

3. **The combined fit ignores T-dependence**: Pooling T=10 and T=20 data without accounting for how the transition shifts with T defeats the purpose of the Power Theory framework. This creates an "average" sigmoid across two fundamentally different transition functions, which obscures the actual T-dependent behavior the theory predicts.

Looking at the actual combined data, I see that α=0.45 maps to both rate=0.0 (T=10) and rate=0.625 (T=20), and α=0.50 appears in both conditions with rate=0.75. Fitting a single sigmoid to these conflicting observations will naturally center around α≈0.45 with moderate steepness, but this conflation of two separate curves is methodologically problematic — the analysis should instead model T as a covariate with T-dependent transition parameters rather than simply pooling the data.

There's also a data integrity concern flagged: α=0.75 with s44=0/8 may not have been re-analyzed after the TY direction fix, so that PermG value could be unreliable. Comparing the earlier locked prediction file to this new analysis, I notice the α_c estimates differ slightly (0.4755 vs 0.4826), and the k values also diverged (200 vs 478), which suggests the earlier fit may have used different exclusion criteria or data points than what I'm working with now. The discrepancy between the earlier fit (α_c=0.4755 with 12 points including α=0.60) and the current one (α_c=0.4826 with 11 points excluding α=0.60) makes sense — dropping that α=0.60 point, which had a near-zero rate after continuity correction, shifts the fitted critical point slightly higher.

Both estimates are essentially indistinguishable given the enormous uncertainty in k (where SE ≈ k itself), so the 0.007 difference is noise. The real problem is that k is completely unconstrained — I can't actually characterize the transition shape, only confirm it's sharp. I'd need much denser sampling around α∈[0.42, 0.48] to pin down the steepness. There's also a methodological concern with pooling T=10 and T=20 data without accounting for temperature dependence, especially since α=0.45 shows zero rate at T=10 but nonzero at T=20. Now I'm moving on to write up the report.

2. The compile_dose_response.py script on the server

5. The discrepancy I'm noticing: alpha_sweep_a060_s43_fp32 uses intermediate naming like K_gte3_3sigma, but the old consensus_k3_3sigma field showed 7/8 while the new robust_pairs_K3 shows 0 — that's a significant mismatch that suggests the cleanup wasn't complete

. The worker claimed these field names weren't found in the registry, but I can see them directly in registry.yaml at lines 4977-4982 for this exact entry, including consensus_k3_3sigma: 7/8 and ty_granger: 4/8. The worker may have been searching for these as standalone fields across the entire registry rather than looking at the specific metrics under this entry.

Looking closer, the real problem isn't whether the worker found them — it's that consensus_k3_3sigma: 7/8 and robust_pairs_K3: 0 both exist for the same experiment, which is a direct conflict. The worker only cleaned up the OLD_* prefixed fields and left these conflicting legacy field names untouched, so the broader issue of duplicate metrics with different values remains unresolved. I'm checking the CSV values I already confirmed earlier to see if the script fix is actually working, then spot-checking several of the 33 rows that supposedly have K3 changes to verify they're correct. The main issue is that a060_s43_fp32 still shows conflicting values between consensus_k3_3sigma and robust_pairs_K3, which suggests the worker may not have fully resolved the dual-field naming conflicts I flagged.

There are still many experiments with old-style field names (ty_granger, K_ge3, K_ge4, consensus_k3_3sigma). The worker only cleaned up the OLD_* prefixed fields in 6 generalize experiments, but didn't address these non-prefixed legacy fields.

The key concern is dual-field conflicts where the old field contradicts the new field. Let me check a specific case: alpha_sweep_a060_s43_fp32 at line 4984.

I already know from earlier reading:
- Line 4978: consensus_k3_3sigma: 7/8 (OLD value, pre-direction-fix)


- Line 5007: robust_pairs_K3: 0 (NEW value, post-direction-fix)

These directly contradict. Any script reading consensus_k3_3sigma would get 7/8 instead of the correct 0. The worker report acknowledges the intermediate naming is distinct from the old format, but that misses the real issue: both fields claim to measure the same thing (K≥3 count) yet report wildly different values.

Looking at the broader picture, there are 47 occurrences of old-style names across the registry, though not all necessarily conflict with new fields—some experiments may only have the old-style fields if they haven't been re-analyzed since the TY fix. The actual problem is when an experiment has both old and new fields with contradicting values. The TY batch rerun worker added new fields to many experiments but didn't remove the old ones, creating this overlap. Given how tangled this is, I'll document it as a remaining issue in my report. The CSV fix checks out (I verified the T=20 values), the OLD_* cleanup is complete, and the non-prefixed old fields are what's left unresolved.

_source line 4514 · 2026-05-20T21:24:28.308Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me review this first-differencing simulation carefully.

**Context**: This is the J2 rebuttal item — a reviewer asked about first-differencing as a robustness check for the Granger causality test. The concern is that levels-based Granger can produce spurious results with non-stationary time series.

**The simulation design**:
- DGP: AR(1) with ar_coef=0.5, T=11 observations, burn-in=50
- 3 effect sizes: β=0.1 (small), β=0.3 (medium), β=0.6 (large)


- Comparing raw levels Granger against first-differenced and permutation-based approaches across 1000 replications with 200 permutations each, measuring power at α=0.05

**Results assessment**: First-differencing substantially reduces power—losing about 39% on average—because with only 11 observations, differencing down to 10 creates a severe loss of information, amplifies noise in the short series, and reduces degrees of freedom in a way that's particularly damaging at this sample size.

Permutation Granger preserves roughly 80% of the raw-levels power, which supports using it over differencing. The empirical effect size from the token entropy to perplexity relationship is Cohen's f²=4.81—extraordinarily large compared to the conventional threshold of 0.35—indicating a very strong causal signal in the data.

**Potential concerns**: The simulation assumes AR(1) dynamics with coefficient 0.5 and Gaussian noise, but the actual metric trajectories might exhibit non-Gaussian innovations with heavy tails, nonlinear dynamics, or time-varying parameters, so these results may not fully generalize. I should also verify that Type I error is properly controlled in the simulation.

Actually, I'm realizing the key issue: permutation Granger has exact Type I error control by design, and first-differencing addresses non-stationarity, but raw-levels Granger on non-stationary data inflates false positives. However, since the DGP uses AR(1) with coefficient 0.5 (which is stationary), the data itself is stationary, so raw Granger's reported power shouldn't be artificially inflated by non-stationarity issues.

But this creates a methodological gap—the simulation only demonstrates that differencing loses power under stationarity, which doesn't address the reviewer's actual concern about what happens if the data *is* non-stationary. I should add a non-stationary scenario with a unit root or near-unit-root coefficient to show how raw Granger's Type I error inflates in that case, while permutation Granger maintains correct size regardless.

On the permutation count, 200 is quite low and creates coarse granularity in the p-values.  With 1000 simulation replicates, the overall power estimate should still be reasonably stable despite the noise from individual permutation tests. The runtime of 15 minutes is acceptable for this scale of simulation.

The core issue is that without testing non-stationarity, the reviewer can push back by saying the reason they asked about differencing in the first place is because the data might not be stationary—so the simulation needs to demonstrate that permutation Granger handles that case while standard Granger fails.

I should flag the missing non-stationary DGP as a methodological gap that needs addressing, but the main finding about power loss from differencing at T=11 is solid and worth reporting. The permutation count of 200 is on the low side but defensible given the computational constraints.
```

{
  "raw": "JSON 已确认写入正确 (n_reps=1000)。汇总结果：\n\n---\n\n### Size (FPR at α=0.05, H0: β=0)\n\n| φ | Raw Granger | First-Diff | Perm Granger |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| 0.50 | **6.4%** | 6.6% | 6.8% |\n| 0.95 | **10.0%** | 5.9% | 9.5% |\n| 0.99 | **11.2%** | 4.2% | 10.7% |\n\n### Power (rejection rate at α=0.05, H1: β>0)\n\n| φ | β | Raw Granger | First-Diff | Perm Granger |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 0.50 | 0.1 | 6.1% | 5.1% | 5.9% |\n| 0.50 | 0.3 | 15.6% | 8.4% | 15.4% |\n| 0.50 | 0.6 | 37.7% | 17.7% | 36.8% |\n| 0.95 | 0.1 | 11.5% | 6.7% | 11.9% |\n| 0.95 | 0.3 | 17.6% | 11.5% | 17.5% |\n| 0.95 | 0.6 | 44.3% | 28.7% | 41.5% |\n| 0.99 | 0.1 | 12.4% | 5.9% | 12.2% |\n| 0.99 | 0.3 | 19.7% | 10.7% | 16.7% |\n| 0.99 | 0.6 | 46.4% | 24.6% | 41.7% |\n\n### 关键发现\n\n1. **Raw Granger FPR 膨胀**：φ=0.99 下 FPR 达 11.2%（是名义 5% 的 2.2 倍），但膨胀幅度不如经典文献中 T→∞ 场景那么极端——T=11 的短序列本身就削弱了检测能力\n2. **First-Diff 保持 size control**：所有 φ 下 FPR 在 4.2%-6.6%，与名义 5% 一致\n3. **Perm Granger 也有 size 膨胀**：φ=0.99 下 FPR=10.7%，因为 permutation 破坏了 x 的自相关结构导致 null distribution 偏移\n4. **Power 很低**：T=11 下即使 β=0.6（大效应），校正后的方法 (first-diff) power 也只有 18-29%\n5. **Size-adjusted 比较**：Raw/Perm 的高 \"power\" 部分来自 size inflation。First-diff 是唯一有 proper size control 的方法"

This is very important! The worker addressed my exact concern about the non-stationary DGP for the J2 rebuttal. Let me read the full artifact.

Key findings:
1. **PermG also has size inflation under near-unit-root**: φ=0.99 → PermG FPR=10.7%. This is a SIGNIFICANT finding because my earlier review assumed PermG would maintain correct size regardless of stationarity (because "permutation test is nonparametric"). But the worker found this isn't true for permutation Granger — the permutation procedure breaks the autocorrelation structure of x, leading to null distribution bias.


2. **First-differencing is the only method with proper size control**: FPR stays at 4.2-6.6% across all φ values, while first-differencing's power is much lower at 18-29% compared to raw Granger's 37-46%.
3. This reframes the entire narrative — PermG's apparent power advantage over first-differencing under stationarity is partially illusory when you account for the size inflation that emerges with near-unit-root processes, so the honest takeaway for the rebuttal needs to acknowledge this tradeoff between maintaining correct size and statistical power.

Now I need to check the full JSON artifact to see the exact numbers and confirm whether the pattern holds across different sample sizes.
```

</details>

```text
1	"""
2	J2 Non-Stationary DGP: Size + Power Simulation
3	Bivariate AR(1) with phi in {0.50, 0.95, 0.99}, T=11
4	Methods: Raw Granger, First-Diff Granger, Permutation Granger
5	"""
6	
7	import json
8	import sys
9	import time
10	import warnings
11	import numpy as np
12	from scipy import stats as sp_stats
13	from tqdm import tqdm
14	
15	warnings.filterwarnings("ignore")
16	
17	SEED = 42
18	T = 11
19	BURN_IN = 50
20	ALPHA = 0.05
21	MAX_LAG = 1
22	PHIS = [0.50, 0.95, 0.99]
23	BETAS_SIZE = [0.0]
24	BETAS_POWER = [0.1, 0.3, 0.6]
25	N_PERMS = 500
26	
27	
28	def generate_bivariate_ar1(phi, beta, T, burn_in, rng):
29	    total = T + burn_in
30	    x = np.zeros(total)
31	    y = np.zeros(total)
32	    eps_x = rng.standard_normal(total)
33	    eps_y = rng.standard_normal(total)
34	    for t in range(1, total):
35	        x[t] = phi * x[t-1] + eps_x[t]
36	        y[t] = phi * y[t-1] + beta * x[t-1] + eps_y[t]
37	    return x[burn_in:], y[burn_in:]
38	
39	
40	def granger_f_stat(x, y, max_lag=1):
41	    """Compute Granger F-stat: does x Granger-cause y? Returns (F, p-value).
42	    Uses direct OLS — much faster than statsmodels VAR for the permutation loop.
43	    """
44	    n = len(x)
45	    # Build lagged data: y[lag:] ~ const + y[lag-1:n-1] + x[lag-1:n-1]
46	    Y = y[max_lag:]
47	    n_obs = len(Y)
48	
49	    # Restricted: Y ~ const + Y_lag
50	    X_r = np.column_stack([np.ones(n_obs), y[max_lag-1:n-1]])
51	    # Unrestricted: Y ~ const + Y_lag + X_lag
52	    X_u = np.column_stack([X_r, x[max_lag-1:n-1]])
53	
54	    # OLS via lstsq
55	    _, rss_r, _, _ = np.linalg.lstsq(X_r, Y, rcond=None)
56	    _, rss_u, _, _ = np.linalg.lstsq(X_u, Y, rcond=None)
57	
58	    # Handle edge case: lstsq may return empty rss for rank-deficient
59	    if len(rss_r) == 0:
60	        rss_r = np.sum((Y - X_r @ np.linalg.lstsq(X_r, Y, rcond=None)[0])**2)
61	    else:
62	        rss_r = rss_r[0]
63	    if len(rss_u) == 0:
64	        rss_u = np.sum((Y - X_u @ np.linalg.lstsq(X_u, Y, rcond=None)[0])**2)
65	    else:
66	        rss_u = rss_u[0]
67	
68	    q = max_lag  # number of restrictions
69	    k_u = X_u.shape[1]  # params in unrestricted
70	    df_denom = n_obs - k_u
71	
72	    if df_denom <= 0 or rss_u <= 0:
73	        return 0.0, 1.0
74	
75	    F = ((rss_r - rss_u) / q) / (rss_u / df_denom)
76	    p = 1.0 - sp_stats.f.cdf(F, q, df_denom)
77	    return F, p
78	
79	
80	def raw_granger_pvalue(x, y, max_lag=1):
81	    _, p = granger_f_stat(x, y, max_lag)
82	    return p
83	
84	
85	def diff_granger_pvalue(x, y, max_lag=1):
86	    dx = np.diff(x)
87	    dy = np.diff(y)
88	    _, p = granger_f_stat(dx, dy, max_lag)
89	    return p
90	
91	
92	def permutation_granger(x, y, n_perms, max_lag, rng):
93	    obs_F, _ = granger_f_stat(x, y, max_lag)
94	
95	    count = 0
96	    for _ in range(n_perms):
97	        x_perm = rng.permutation(x)
98	        perm_F, _ = granger_f_stat(x_perm, y, max_lag)
99	        if perm_F >= obs_F:
100	            count += 1
101	
102	    return (count + 1) / (n_perms + 1)
103	
104	
105	def run_simulation(n_reps, n_perms, phis, betas, seed, label=""):
106	    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
107	    results = {}
108	
109	    total_conditions = len(phis) * len(betas)
110	    condition_idx = 0
111	
112	    for phi in phis:
113	        results[str(phi)] = {}
114	        for beta in betas:
115	            condition_idx += 1
116	            desc = f"{label} phi={phi}, beta={beta} [{condition_idx}/{total_conditions}]"
117	
118	            raw_reject = 0
119	            diff_reject = 0
120	            perm_reject = 0
121	            raw_fail = 0
122	            diff_fail = 0
123	            perm_fail = 0
124	
125	            for _ in tqdm(range(n_reps), desc=desc, leave=True):
126	                x, y = generate_bivariate_ar1(phi, beta, T, BURN_IN, rng)
127	
128	                try:
129	                    p = raw_granger_pvalue(x, y, MAX_LAG)
130	                    if p < ALPHA:
131	                        raw_reject += 1
132	                except Exception:
133	                    raw_fail += 1
134	
135	                try:
136	                    p = diff_granger_pvalue(x, y, MAX_LAG)
137	                    if p < ALPHA:
138	                        diff_reject += 1
139	                except Exception:
140	                    diff_fail += 1
141	
142	                try:
143	                    p = permutation_granger(x, y, n_perms, MAX_LAG, rng)
144	                    if p < ALPHA:
145	                        perm_reject += 1
146	                except Exception:
147	                    perm_fail += 1
148	
149	            valid_raw = n_reps - raw_fail
150	            valid_diff = n_reps - diff_fail
151	            valid_perm = n_reps - perm_fail
152	
153	            results[str(phi)][str(beta)] = {
154	                "raw_rate": round(raw_reject / valid_raw, 4) if valid_raw > 0 else None,
155	                "diff_rate": round(diff_reject / valid_diff, 4) if valid_diff > 0 else None,
156	                "perm_rate": round(perm_reject / valid_perm, 4) if valid_perm > 0 else None,
157	                "raw_fail": raw_fail,
158	                "diff_fail": diff_fail,
159	                "perm_fail": perm_fail,
160	                "n_reps": n_reps,
161	            }
162	            print(f"  -> Raw={results[str(phi)][str(beta)]['raw_rate']}, "
163	                  f"Diff={results[str(phi)][str(beta)]['diff_rate']}, "
164	                  f"Perm={results[str(phi)][str(beta)]['perm_rate']}")
165	
166	    return results
167	
168	
169	def main():
170	    dry_run = "--dry-run" in sys.argv
171	    n_reps = 10 if dry_run else 1000
172	    n_perms = 50 if dry_run else N_PERMS
173	
174	    print(f"{'DRY RUN (10 reps, 50 perms)' if dry_run else 'FULL RUN (1000 reps, 500 perms)'}")
175	    print(f"T={T}, burn_in={BURN_IN}, alpha={ALPHA}, seed={SEED}")
176	    print("=" * 60)
177	
178	    t0 = time.time()
179	
180	    print("\n=== Part A: Size Simulation (H0: beta=0) ===")
181	    size_results = run_simulation(n_reps, n_perms, PHIS, BETAS_SIZE, SEED, label="Size")
182	
183	    print("\n=== Part B: Power Simulation (H1: beta>0) ===")
184	    power_results = run_simulation(n_reps, n_perms, PHIS, BETAS_POWER, SEED + 1, label="Power")
185	
186	    elapsed = time.time() - t0
187	    print(f"\nTotal time: {elapsed/60:.1f} minutes")
188	
189	    size_formatted = {}
190	    for phi in PHIS:
191	        d = size_results[str(phi)]["0.0"]
192	        size_formatted[str(phi)] = {
193	            "raw_fpr": d["raw_rate"],
194	            "diff_fpr": d["diff_rate"],
195	            "perm_fpr": d["perm_rate"],
196	            "raw_fail_count": d["raw_fail"],
197	            "diff_fail_count": d["diff_fail"],
198	            "perm_fail_count": d["perm_fail"],
199	        }
200	
201	    power_formatted = {}
202	    for phi in PHIS:
203	        power_formatted[str(phi)] = {}
204	        for beta in BETAS_POWER:
205	            d = power_results[str(phi)][str(beta)]
206	            power_formatted[str(phi)][str(beta)] = {
207	                "raw_power": d["raw_rate"],
208	                "diff_power": d["diff_rate"],
209	                "perm_power": d["perm_rate"],
210	                "raw_fail_count": d["raw_fail"],
211	                "diff_fail_count": d["diff_fail"],
212	                "perm_fail_count": d["perm_fail"],
213	            }
214	
215	    raw_95 = size_formatted["0.95"]["raw_fpr"]
216	    raw_99 = size_formatted["0.99"]["raw_fpr"]
217	    diff_95 = size_formatted["0.95"]["diff_fpr"]
218	    diff_99 = size_formatted["0.99"]["diff_fpr"]
219	    perm_95 = size_formatted["0.95"]["perm_fpr"]
220	    perm_99 = size_formatted["0.99"]["perm_fpr"]
221	
222	    conclusion = (
223	        f"Size control: Raw Granger FPR inflates to {raw_95} (phi=0.95) and {raw_99} (phi=0.99), "
224	        f"confirming spurious rejection under non-stationarity. "
225	        f"First-diff Granger maintains FPR at {diff_95}/{diff_99}, "
226	        f"and Permutation Granger at {perm_95}/{perm_99}, "
227	        f"both near nominal 5%. "
228	        f"Power: See power_results for beta-specific detection rates across phi values."
229	    )
230

233	            "T": T,
234	            "burn_in": BURN_IN,
235	            "n_reps": n_reps,
236	            "n_perms": n_perms,

240	            "alpha": ALPHA,
241	        },

246	    }
247	
248	    out_path = "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/j2_nonstationary_size_power.json"

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me do a thorough review of the simulation code and results.

**DGP**: Bivariate AR(1)
- x[t] = φ * x[t-1] + ε_x[t]
- y[t] = φ * y[t-1] + β * x[t-1] + ε_y[t]

Both x and y have the SAME AR coefficient φ. This is important — both series have the same persistence. Under H0 (β=0), x and y are independent AR(1) processes.

**Key issue with the permutation test**: The permutation procedure shuffles x randomly (line 97: `x_perm = rng.permutation(x)`). 

This destroys the autocorrelation structure of x, which is the real problem. The Granger F-test compares a restricted model (y[t] regressed on just y[t-1]) against an unrestricted model (y[t] regressed on y[t-1] and x[t-1]). Under the true null where β=0, the test statistic should follow a known distribution, but when I permute x, I'm breaking its temporal dependence and creating a false null distribution that doesn't match the actual data-generating process.

The core issue is that when φ is close to 1, y becomes highly persistent and the restricted model already explains most of the variance. The autocorrelated x can then spuriously correlate with the residuals—the classic spurious regression trap. But by shuffling x into white noise for the permutation test, I'm asking "how much would a random predictor help?" rather than "how much would this specific autocorrelated process help?" A highly autocorrelated x has more apparent predictive power just from its persistence, so the permutation null distribution is fundamentally misaligned with what the actual test is measuring.

Now looking at the size results: at φ=0.50 (stationary), all three methods show FPR around 6-7%, which is slightly elevated above 5% but within the margin of error given the standard error of roughly 0.007.

When φ=0.95 (near-unit-root), the raw and permutation methods jump to 10% and 9.5% respectively—roughly double the nominal level and clearly outside the acceptable confidence interval—while first-differencing brings it down to 5.9%. At φ=0.99, this pattern intensifies with raw and permutation methods reaching 11.2% and 10.7%.

For power, I need to account for the fact that the raw and permutation methods' power estimates are contaminated by size inflation under non-stationarity.

Now I'm reviewing the code implementation: the permutation test correctly shuffles the entire x series, the Granger F-statistic uses proper OLS with the right degrees of freedom (T-4 for the raw series, T-6 for differenced), and the first-differencing approach...

Applying differencing before the Granger test is the right order. The DGP setup looks standard with one-directional coupling and independent seeds for size and power simulations, plus I've bumped the permutation count to 500.

But there are some methodological gaps worth noting: the simulation assumes both x and y share the same AR coefficient, which doesn't match reality where canary metrics and downstream metrics likely have different persistence patterns. Also, I haven't actually tested whether the real experimental data exhibits non-stationarity—the simulation just shows what would happen if it did.

The critical issue is that PermG doesn't fully eliminate the non-stationarity problem, especially at extreme persistence levels like φ=0.99. For more moderate persistence (φ=0.5-0.9), it performs acceptably. And there's a discrepancy in the JSON conclusion claiming 10.7% is "near nominal 5%"—that's clearly wrong, and the raw summary correctly flags this inconsistency. I'm seeing that the permutation Granger test also shows size inflation under near-unit-root conditions. The simulation reveals a critical trade-off: permutation Granger maintains correct size under stationarity but inflates false positive rates around 10% when data is highly persistent, while first-differencing keeps size correct but loses power. For the rebuttal, I should acknowledge this inflation issue, argue their actual data likely sits in the moderately persistent range rather than near unit-root, and ideally reference ADF test results on their empirical data to support that claim.
```

121	  "conclusion": "Raw AR(1) coefficients split into two regimes: (1) Under the null/low-alpha (a=0.00-0.50), token_entropy phi ranges [-0.51, -0.10] — anti-correlated or near zero, far below 0.95. (2) At a=0.75-1.00, token_entropy phi reaches 0.14-1.00, but this reflects the deterministic collapse trend (the signal itself), not stochastic persistence. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is the empirical AR(1) analysis I recommended in my J2 nonstationary simulation review. Let me assess it carefully.

**What the analysis does**: Estimates AR(1) coefficients for token_entropy and distinct_1 series across 7 experiments (5 T=10 + 2 T=20), both on raw levels and first differences.

**Key findings**:

1. **token_entropy raw φ**:
   - null/low α (0.00-0.50): φ ∈ [-0.51, -0.10] — NEGATIVE or near-zero autocorrelation. Far from unit root.
   - high α (0.75-1.00): φ ≈ 0.14-0.996 — but this reflects the collapse trend itself, not genuine stochastic persistence.

2. **distinct_1 raw φ**:
   - Uniformly high (0.81-0.97) across all alpha levels — initially looks problematic.
   - After differencing though: φ drops to an average of 0.17 with range [-0.14, 0.44] — the increments behave like white noise.

3. **Worker's interpretation**: The high raw φ is driven by deterministic trend, not stochastic autocorrelation.

Now I'm thinking through the critical distinction between deterministic and stochastic persistence. The worker's right that distinct_1's elevated raw φ stems from a monotonic downward trend—even in the null condition, iterative training causes a slight diversity decline. Once I difference the series to remove that trend, φ collapses to around 0.17, which confirms the residuals are essentially white noise. But here's the subtlety: PermG operates on raw levels, not differenced data, so I need to check whether the permutation procedure actually handles deterministic trends correctly. When PermG shuffles the cause variable, it destroys any trend structure in that variable, which under the null hypothesis should matter—but I need to think through whether this invalidates the test or actually makes it more conservative.

The mechanism is becoming clearer: if both x and y share a deterministic trend (from the same iterative training process), the observed F-statistic will be inflated due to spurious correlation from that shared trend. When I permute x, I strip away its trend structure, so the permuted F-statistics become smaller, making the observed statistic look artificially extreme. This is the same FPR inflation I saw in the non-stationary simulations, just driven by deterministic rather than stochastic trends.

The null experiment is the key diagnostic here. At α=0.00 with no synthetic data and no causal mechanism, distinct_1 shows φ_raw = 0.86 and φ_diff = -0.14, meaning there's a strong trend present even in the null condition itself. Token_entropy, by contrast, shows φ_raw = -0.51 and φ_diff = -0.57, with no trend issue. So distinct_1's trend isn't an artifact of the causal mechanism—it's baked into the null data generation process.

Yet the empirical null FPR is 0/24 across all seed and pair combinations in the α=0 condition, which is the strongest evidence that this theoretical concern doesn't actually materialize in practice. The trend structure exists, but PermG isn't spuriously rejecting the null. And with only T=10 observations, individual autocorrelation estimates have wide confidence intervals (standard error around 0.3), so sampling variability is substantial and could be masking or dampening any trend-driven inflation.

Looking at the signal cases, the autocorrelation persists even after differencing — token_entropy at α=0.75 has φ=0.88 post-differencing, and at α=1.00 it jumps to φ=0.976, indicating the collapse trajectory itself has strong momentum. But these are the cases where PermG should be detecting a signal, so high autocorrelation in the differenced series is actually expected behavior for power, not a false positive problem.

The rebuttal's core logic holds: under the null, both metrics stay well below the φ=0.95 danger threshold, and the empirical 0/24 false positive rate confirms size control. Under the signal, the high raw autocorrelation reflects genuine causal structure rather than stochastic noise. The one wrinkle is that distinct_1 already shows φ=0.86 in the null case, which the simulation suggests could yield around 7-8% false positive rate — but the empirical evidence constrains this to acceptable levels, even if the theoretical argument alone leaves some ambiguity.
```

### Assistant

_source line 4662 · 2026-05-20T23:13:24.792Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Clear root cause: The experiment failed because it couldn't load the Pythia-1B model from HuggingFace. The error is:

```
RuntimeError: Cannot send a request, as the client has been closed.
OSError: Can't load the model for 'EleutherAI/pythia-1b'
```

This is a network/HF Hub issue on westd-19828. The model isn't cached locally (or the cache is corrupted), and the attempt to download it failed because the HTTP client was closed.

The fix would be:
1. Check if the model is cached: `ls /root/autodl-tmp/.hf_cache/models--EleutherAI--pythia-1b/`


2. If not cached, download it first with proper network settings (source /etc/network_turbo for AutoDL)
3. Set HF_HOME and TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE appropriately

Looking at the spec command, it doesn't set HF_HOME or TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE, unlike other experiments on westd-17855. The command just runs `python -u iterative_train.py` without these environment variables, which is likely the issue — I should add `export HF_HOME=/root/autodl-tmp/.hf_cache && export TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE=1` to match the setup on the working machine.

Comparing to the a060_s43 experiment which properly exports HF_HOME, TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE, and HF_HUB_OFFLINE before activating conda and running the training script, the failed experiment is missing all this environment configuration. Without HF_HOME set, it defaults to ~/.cache/huggingface, and without TRANSFORMERS_OFFLINE, it tries to download models — which fails on 19828 since network_turbo isn't available.

The wall clock time of only 393 seconds and an output directory containing just EXIT_CODE and config.json confirms the model never loaded. This is a straightforward environment setup issue, not a data problem. The existing metrics in the registry are from a previous successful run of the v1 experiment, while this v2 rerun was submitted fresh at 23:05 UTC and failed during model initialization.
````

</details>

### Assistant

_source line 4695 · 2026-05-20T23:22:33.102Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Clear OOM failure:
- Pythia-6.9B on a single 96GB GPU
- Model + optimizer states consume ~94.72 GiB
- Failed at optimizer.step() when trying to allocate exp_avg_sq (Adam moment)
- gen_0 and gen_1 directories exist — it completed gen_0 (baseline, no training) and possibly gen_1, but OOM'd during gen_1 training

Pythia-6.9B in fp32:
- Model params: 6.9B × 4 bytes = 27.6 GB
- Adam optimizer: 2 moments × 27.6 GB = 55.2 GB  


- Activations and gradients add another ~12 GB on top, pushing the total to around 95 GB — right at the limit even with gradient checkpointing and batch size 2

The fp32 precision is just too heavy for a single 96GB GPU. I should look back at those earlier successful Pythia-6.9B runs to see what precision they were using.

Looking at the registry, the earlier experiments like `pythia69b_wiki_a100_s42` and `pythia69b_wiki_a100_s43` didn't have the _fp32 suffix, which suggests they probably ran in bf16 instead. That would cut the model memory roughly in half — from 27.6 GB down to around 13.8 GB — and would be the most straightforward fix since those experiments already proved it works. Let me dig into what those previous commands actually used.
```

</details>

Let me now compare with the s42 reference experiment (interv_react_s42_fp32). The hypothesis says: "Validates s42 result (trigger Gen 2, recovery Gen 4) is reproducible across seeds."

**s43 results:**

| Gen | token_entropy | distinct_1 | perplexity | ece | effective_alpha | intervention_triggered |
|-----|--------------|-----------|-----------|-----|-----------------|----------------------|
| 0 | 3.234 | 0.098 | 26.95 | 0.011 | 1.0 | false |


| 2 | 1.403 | 0.083 | 158.22 | 0.329 | 1.0 | true (trigger_gen=2) |
| 4 | 3.085 | 0.085 | 21.55 | 0.013 | 0.0 | true |
| 10 | 3.088 | 0.083 | 21.39 | 0.015 | 0.0 | true |

The s43 results confirm the key pattern from s42: the intervention triggers at generation 2 as expected, and by generation 4 the model recovers with effective_alpha dropping to 0, showing the intervention successfully suppressed the problematic behavior. Through gen 10, the metrics stabilize near their recovered values, with token_entropy and perplexity staying close to baseline while distinct_1 remains about 15% below baseline. This confirms the intervention reproducibly triggers at gen 2 and recovers by gen 4 across both seeds, validating the s42 result.

The key detail is that gen 2 was trained with α=1.0 before the intervention kicked in, so starting from gen 3 the model uses α=0.0 — this clean separation supports the mitigation narrative. Now I'm checking the exit code to confirm the run completed successfully.
```
